abstract_en: This thesis introduces an innovative approach that utilizes wearable
  biosensors, low-cost eye-tracking technology, and machine learning to assess user
  comprehension and engagement at local levels of content. It addresses the significant
  challenge of identifying “when and where” users’ comprehension difficulties occur
  on screens identified by changes in Autonomic Nervous System (ANS) activity, such
  as heart rate variability (HRV) and Electrodermal Activity (EDA). The core concept,
  which we coined "TellBack," combines non-intrusive biosensors with eye tracking
  to assess cognitive load using ANS modulations and localize where comprehension
  challenges occur within digital content. This approach demonstrates promising classification
  accuracy for predicting difficulties and low engagement perceived by individuals
  in content regions compared to existing methods. The work further extends the core
  concept to two distinct application domains. The first application, "iReview," is
  developed for the software industry and aims to enhance code inspection (review)
  by evaluating the reviewer's understanding and engagement with the code under review.
  Using machine learning, we estimate the surrogate of cognitive load and classify
  code regions based on code comprehension, providing insights into reviewers’ performance
  and potentially improving the quality of code reviews by identifying code regions
  that were not well reviewed. The second application, "iMind," focuses on improving
  foreign language comprehension in educational and training settings. In this application,
  the goal is to predict comprehension difficulties within English texts, making it
  possible to provide personalized and contextualized help for nonnative speakers
  at local levels of text (e.g., paragraphs), by integrating different cognitive load
  manifestations encoded in the ANS using wearables and contextual information, "iMind"
  effectively identifies "hotspots" in texts that present comprehension challenges,
  thereby opening the possibility to develop cognitively aware interfaces for learning
  purposes.
source_url: https://estudogeral.uc.pt/handle/10316/117181

ix

Abstract
T
his thesis introduces an innovative approach that utilizes wearable bi-
osensors, low-cost eye-tracking technology, and machine learning to
assess user comprehension and engagement at local levels of content.
It addresses the significant challenge of identifying “when and where” users’
comprehension difficulties occur on screens identified by changes in Autonomic
Nervous System (ANS) activity, such as heart rate variability (HRV) and Elec-
trodermal Activity (EDA). The core concept, which we coined ”TellBack,” com-
bines non-intrusive biosensors with eye tracking to assess cognitive load using
ANS modulations and localize where comprehension challenges occur within di-
gital content. This approach demonstrates promising classification accuracy for
predicting difficulties and low engagement perceived by individuals in content re-
gions compared to existing methods. The work further extends the core concept
to two distinct application domains. The first application, ”iReview,” is de-
veloped for the software industry and aims to enhance code inspection (review)
by evaluating the reviewer’s understanding and engagement with the code un-
der review. Using machine learning, we estimate the surrogate of cognitive load
and classify code regions based on code comprehension, providing insights into
reviewers’ performance and potentially improving the quality of code reviews by
identifying code regions that were not well reviewed. The second application,
”iMind,” focuses on improving foreign language comprehension in educational
and training settings. In this application, the goal is to predict comprehension
difficulties within English texts, making it possible to provide personalized and
contextualized help for nonnative speakers at local levels of text (e.g., para-
graphs), by integrating different cognitive load manifestations encoded in the
ANS using wearables and contextual information, ”iMind” effectively identifies
”hotspots” in texts that present comprehension challenges, thereby opening the
possibility to develop cognitively aware interfaces for learning purposes.
Keywords: Artificial Intelligence, Biofeedback Sensors, Cognitive Load,
Content Comprehension, Software Engineering.
xi

Resumo
E
sta dissertação propõe uma abordagem inovadora para a avaliação da
compreensão de conteúdos digitais com recurso a biossensores, tecno-
logias de monitorização ocular (eye-trackers) e aprendizagem computa-
cional. Em particular, abordam-se dois desafios fundamentais relacionados com
a identificação no espaço e no tempo das dificuldades de compreensão per-
cecionadas por utilizadores em a conteúdos digitais, recorrendo para o efeito à
identificação de modulações da carga cognitiva veiculadas pelo sistema nervoso
autónomo (SNA). O conceito central a que chamamos ”TellBack”, combina bios-
sensores não invasivos para avaliar variações de carga cognitiva e permitir iden-
tificar os instantes críticos à compreensão, com a monitorização ocular para in-
dicar a localização onde esses mesmos desafios ocorrem no conteúdo digital. Os
testes realizados em contextos distintos demonstram que a abordagem permite
alcançar uma precisão na localização tempo-espacial bastante promissora o que
abre possibilidades de intervenção automatizadas de apoio ao utilizador com um
potencial muito significativo. Observa-se ainda baixos níveis de intrusividade
percecionada pelos utilizadores em comparação com métodos alternativos exist-
entes. Nesta dissertação demonstramos o conceito “TellBack” em dois domínios
de aplicação distintos: a primeira instanciação, que designamos ”iReview”, visa
apoiar as tarefas de revisão de código, avaliando a qualidade de tarefas de in-
speção de código. Nesta aplicação, propõe-se uma solução por recurso a uma
abordagembaseadaemaprendizagemcomputacionalque, recorrendoàsmedidas
de carga cognitiva, permite identificar regiões de código onde os níveis de com-
preensão não são satisfatórios. Isto permite aferir o desempenho dos revisores
e a qualidade das revisões de código, podendo contribuir de forma significativa
para a melhoria do código produzido. A segunda instância, que designamos
”iMind”, foca-se na compreensão de idiomas estrangeiros em contextos educa-
cionais. Nesta aplicação, o objetivo é prever dificuldades de compreensão em
textos em inglês, fornecendo ajuda personalizada e contextualizada a não nat-
ivos. A solução ”iMind” permite identificar os pontos críticos à compreensão do
conteúdo, melhorando assim a experiência de aprendizagem.
Palavras-chave: Inteligência Artificial, Sensores de Biofeedback, Carga
Cognitiva, Compreensão de Conteúdo, Engenharia de Software.
xiii

Foreword
T
he work detailed in this thesis was accomplished at the Laboratory of
Software and Systems (SSE) and Adaptive Computation of the Center
for Informatics and Systems of the University of Coimbra (CISUC), in
collaborationwiththeInstituteforNuclearSciencesAppliedtoHealth(ICNAS),
within the context of the following projects and grants:
BASE - Biofeedback Augmented Software Engineering (POCI - 01-0145 -
FEDER- 031581). This project investigates software bugs through an inter-
disciplinary lens, combining neuroscience, physiological responses, and software
reliability engineering. It aims to understand the brain mechanisms in error-
making and discovery during programming, focusing on software quality im-
provement through biofeedback-augmented software engineering in three main
phases.
AI4EU - AI4EU establishes a European AI-on-demand platform, uniting the
ecosystem with 80 partners. It fosters innovation, technology transfer, and SME
growth, serving as a comprehensive broker. The platform, built upon existing
AI components, includes industry-driven pilots and research to stimulate innov-
ation. UC focuses on Verifiable AI, researching safety and security in AI-based
systems.
V ALU3S- VALU3S streamlines automated system verification and validation
(V &V) by evaluating methods and tools. The project creates a multi-domain
framework to reduce time and costs for V &V related to safety, cyber-security,
and privacy. The international consortium comprises 25 industrial partners, 6
research institutes, and 10 universities, aiming to achieve efficiency in the V&V
process.
PhD grant - Foundation for Science and Technology (FCT)
(2022.11880.BD).

The culmination of the research described in this thesis has led to the submission
of a patent application titled ”Method and system for scoring a software review
process” (Application filed by Universidade De Coimbra, PCT/IB2021/059461).
This patent application, made through the World Intellectual Property Organiz-
ation (WIPO) in Geneva, Switzerland, involves a collaborative effort by multiple
authors, including Haytham W. I. Hijazi, Henrique S. C. Madeira, Ricardo J.
D. S. Couceiro, Paulo F. Pereira de Carvalho, Miguel Sá Sousa Castelo Branco,
João M. Seabra Castelhano, Júlio Cordeiro Medeiros, Raul André Brajczewski
Barbosa, and João António P. Almeida Durães. The innovative method and
system proposed in the patent application focus on scoring software review pro-
cesses, incorporating elements such as Software Engineering, Software Reviews,
Code Reviews, Code Quality, Software Testing, Biometrics, and Intelligent Con-
tent Comprehension. More details can be found in the Appendix.

land in March 2023. The talk, titled ”Navigating the Challenges of Cognitive
Load Monitoring for Content Comprehension Prediction with Wearable Devices
and Machine Learning,” provided an opportunity to share insights and findings
from my Ph.D. work.

Chapter 1
Introduction
— 1 —

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
By comprehending a given piece of content, readers can assess the author’s
arguments, analyze the supporting evidence, and develop their critical thinking
skills. This skill is crucial for problem solving, decision-making, and intellectual
discourse []. In the acclaimed work ”How to Solve It” by [Polya, 2004], the
primary emphasis is placed on the critical importance of understanding the
context. Individuals can approach solutions more effectively if they have an
in-depth comprehension of the context, constraints, and underlying principles
of the problem. This emphasis on comprehending ensures that problem solvers
focus on understanding important information to help prevent errors.
In contrast, comprehension difficulties, in various contexts, can have dramatic
consequences. For example, [TEA, 2002] reports that difficulties in comprehen-
sion can cause frustration and lack of confidence among students. Furthermore,
in a recent study that focused on eye movements to predict reading difficulties,
[Bingel et al., 2018] stated that reading comprehension difficulties can hinder
individuals’ ability to access written materials, subsequently leading to reduced
self-confidence and poor learning performance. For example, children with learn-
ing disabilities (LD) and special educational needs, who encounter challenges in
text comprehension, together with other difficulties, may face an elevated risk
of academic and life failures [Woolley and Woolley, 2011].
Comprehension is a crucial aspect, not only in educational and pedagogical
contexts but also in major industries like software development, with a specific
focus on code or software comprehension. Difficulties in comprehending code
can significantly impact the process of software maintenance, which is already
known for its time-consuming, costly, and challenging nature [Fakhoury, 2018].
Even with the rapid growth of AI tools capable of generating and potentially
explainingcode, it remainscrucial to comprehend thecode and softwareartifacts
produced by these tools, like ChatGPT or Copilot, to enhance trust and reliance
on them.
Reading from electronic screens has become an integral part of modern society,
anditsintegrationintoeducation, learningmethodologies, andvariousindustries
is on the rise [Delgado and Salmerón, 2022]. Within this evolving landscape of
reading practices, our study is driven by the need to assess and predict compre-
hension and engagement challenges and difficulties during screen-based inform-
ation processing comprehensively and objectively. According to Baron’s work
reported in [Baron, 2021], people have adapted to the wealth of readily available
information by leaning towards skimming text rather than engaging in thor-
ough reading. This mode of reading, while posing challenges to comprehension,
can yield unfavorable consequences, depending on the content’s criticality. Such
consequences encompass declined work quality, less effective decision-making
[Finucane et al., 2005], and reduced productivity [Dermitzaki et al., 2008].
When acquiring or updating knowledge, skills, or values during comprehension,
itisvitaltomanagethecognitiveloadtofitwithintheconstraintsofourworking
memory [Lee, 2014]. Cognitive load refers to the mental effort required when
people perform cognitive tasks [Staal, 2004]. It is a crucial factor in areas such as
— 2 —

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
healthcare, education, the software industry, aviation, and military command.
High cognitive load can lead to stress, errors, and limits in performing tasks
when they are too demanding for our working memory.
One of the key theories addressing cognitive load and its association with task
complexity, contentpresentation, andlearningparadigmsisCognitiveLoadThe-
ory (CLT) [Sweller, 2011]. CLT identifies intrinsic, extraneous, and germane
components as the respective factors influencing these aspects. The theory pos-
its that our working memory has limited capacity and duration for handling
new information, allowing us to process only a limited amount of information
simultaneously [Sweller et al., 2019]. This characteristic of working memory
supports the notion that increased cognitive load can be linked to reading com-
prehension difficulties [DeStefano and LeFevre, 2007]. However, the relationship
between cognitive load variations and comprehension levels is complex and in-
fluenced by multiple factors, including task complexity, prior knowledge, and
reading or learning strategies, as per CLT. Consequently, assessing cognitive
load while considering these factors is crucial in various learning and industrial
domains.
The most widely reported methods for measuring cognitive load are subjective
measures based on mental effort and task difficulty ratings [Sweller et al., 2019].
Subjective measures, although simple to implement, assume that learners or
subjects have a clear understanding of what ”invested mental effort” and ”task
difficulty” mean. Thus, the reliability and validity of such ratings are question-
able.
On the other hand, measuring cognitive load using objective methods, includ-
ing neurophysiological responses such as electroencephalography (EEG), pupil
dilation, eye movement, or Heart Rate Variability (HRV), provides continuous
information throughout biosensors or commercial wearables, enabling research-
ers to capture cognitive load changes while individuals are performing mentally
demanding tasks, such as reading comprehension, which is our focus in this
thesis. Such objective methods to capture the cognitive load eliminate the need
for interruptions in the learning process, which is common when relying on sub-
jective assessments of comprehension [Korbach et al., 2017].
Thus, the need for new methods to assess cognitive load and monitor during
mentally demanding tasks is crucial. Assessing an individual’s mental state can
significantly improve interactions and user experience in modern autonomous
systems. For example, the work of [Chen et al., 2016] showed that high levels
of cognitive load, often referred to as ”cognitive overload”, can have unwanted
effects on working memory performance. In contrast, insufficient levels of cog-
nitive load, termed ”cognitive underload,” may lead to suboptimal performance
due to factors such as boredom or a lack of motivation [Young et al., 2014]. This
emphasizes the importance of continuous cognitive load evaluation in critical,
intellectual, and mentally demanding tasks.
To give a simple example of the benefits of assessing cognitive load, let us con-
sider the following application: measuring cognitive load and engagement of in-
dividuals in an adaptive learning platform. The platform content and material
— 3 —

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
could be tailored to the student’s cognitive state [Troussas et al., 2020; Mwambe,
2024] captured through wearable sensors. In Mwambe’s work [Mwambe, 2024],
the author proposes an adaptive e-learning system that addresses time con-
straints and work overload by dynamically personalizing learning content based
on learners’ cognitive processes and states, improving adaptive decision-making
and learning efficacy.
The efficient assessment of cognitive load, both cost-effective and reasonably
accurate, relies on the utilization of biofeedback wearable sensors capable of
capturing physiological signals such as HRV and EDA. These signals reflect the
modulations in the Autonomic Nervous System (ANS), which includes the sym-
pathetic and parasympathetic nervous systems. For instance, an elevated heart
rate corresponds to increased sympathetic activity, whereas parasympathetic
activity leads to a decrease in heart rate. In essence, the sympathetic compon-
ent facilitates actions in response to stress or emergencies, often referred to as
’fight-or-flight’ responses, while the parasympathetic component regulates activ-
ities related to relaxation and digestion, known as the ’rest-and-digest’ functions
[McCorry, 2007]. Performing mentally demanding tasks have the ability to trig-
ger certain changes in the patterns of the ANS, which are our key focus in the
measurement.
This technological advancement in wearable sensors, coupled with advanced
signal processing and machine learning capabilities, has sparked a revolution
in behavior and cognitive monitoring across various domains, including educa-
tion, software development, healthcare, and work related to human well-being
[Gjoreski et al., 2021].
In addition to wearable devices, other measures that assess the cognitive load,
attention, or behavior extend to other technologies, such as eye trackers. Eye-
tracking devices are capable of monitoring a person’s gaze and can measure
parameters like pupil diameter and dilation as a response [ Wierzbicki and
Plechawska-Wójcik, 2023]. Eye trackers are very relevant devices in this context
since they can simultaneously provide screen spatial coordinate information, in-
dicating where the user is looking. Since eye-trackers provide timestamps for
each gaze point, it is possible to synchronize their data with the biofeedback
wearable devices and sensors to know exactly when and where the difficulty
on the screen occurs. Moreover, eye-trackers provide more advanced features
than just coordinates, including pupil diameter variations, which have shown
their capacity to index cognitive load changes in reading and comprehension
processes. Thus, the integration of eye-tracking technology with advanced data
analysis techniques provides insights into cognitive processes that complement
the information obtained from wearable sensors.
Given all these available techniques to capture cognitive load changes, the work
in this thesis takes into account that the neurophysiological responses, particu-
larly the HRV, are not specific to a mental task and might be triggered by other
confounding sources. Therefore, the developed models in this thesis integrated
several contextual information that correlate with the comprehension process to
minimize this measurement uncertainty, which complies with the CLT.
— 4 —

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
Capturing comprehension difficulties quantitatively is a complex task. To over-
comethischallenge, wehavetrainedstate-of-the-artmachinelearningalgorithms
using an integrated feature space comprising physiological and contextual vari-
ables. Moreover, as detailed in Chapter 5 and Chapter 6, the work utilized com-
pound formulas for labeling the data points based on ground-truth information,
including participants’ annotations (difficult & uncertain) and comprehension
answers at the local level of content. With the integration of the individual’s
cognition and performance, prior knowledge, and task characteristics, it was pos-
sible to build a machine learning model that could utilize these data and perform
the necessary interaction between complex features to model the comprehension
difficulty at local levels of content.
Figure 1.1 shows an example of one of the prototype tools developed to evaluate
the accuracy and effectiveness of the proposed approach. Chapter 4 and Chapter
6 discuss the realization of the concept in prototype-like tools in two different do-
mains. To summarize, this thesis presents a novel method to assess and predict
Figure 1.1: An experimental prototype of the approach.
comprehension challenges and engagement in digital content by analyzing in-
dividuals’ physiological signals and eye movements through a machine-learning
pipeline to pinpoint challenging regions in content. This proof of concept is
covered in Chapter 2 of this thesis. One of the important implications of this
work is the ability to utilize the model developed to build an application that
offers real-time assistance during the reading and comprehension process.
We evaluated this approach using non-invasive, accurate biosensors and eye
trackers and verified its accuracy using reference measurements like EEG. We
also applied this concept to two distinct complex problems that are discussed
in Chapter 4 and Chapter 5, respectively: software review quality evaluation
based on code comprehension as well as predicting natural language compre-
hension difficulties. In these applications, we utilized cost-effective wearables
— 5 —

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
like smartwatches along with desktop eye trackers and assessed their effective-
ness.
1.1 Objectives
The principal objective of this thesis is todevelop and evaluate an intel-
ligent approach that can use wearable technology and non-invasive
biosensors to assess content comprehension and engagement levels at
the digital content’s local level . Therefore, the following objectives have
been established:
• Examine the use of non-invasive biosensors to assess cognitive load asso-
ciated with comprehension difficulties;
• Investigate the role of eye-tracker to pinpoint the hotspots in the content
regions that are associated with high cognitive load.
• Build a machine learning-based model that can predict comprehension
difficulties at the regional level of content.
• Apply the model concept in two distinct applications, namely in software
comprehension and review and natural language comprehension. This
objective demonstrates the ability of the concept to have a concrete impact
in realistic environments and knowledge fields.
Non-invasive biosensors are examined using two main approaches: first, medical-
based high-precision biosensors are used for proof of concept, and second, com-
mercial wearables and wristbands are utilized to investigate if it is possible to
instantiate a prototype tool in various applications.
Eye trackers have also been used in two primary ways: first, to extract gaze
information such as fixation duration, reading time, and the number of regres-
sions or revisits to certain areas of the content; second, to detect the places
where the person was gazing that corresponded to high cognitive load. For the
evaluation, in this work, generalizable machine learning models were developed.
These models can handle fine-grained content regions and classify them as ”dif-
ficult” or ”non-difficult” based on the available features—a topic that will be
covered in different chapters in this thesis.
1.2 Global Contributions
Taking into consideration the goals described above, this thesis has produced
the following global contributions:
• Contribution 1: Using inexpensive biosensors, wearables, and
machine learning models, this thesis presented a novel ap-
proach to evaluating understanding and engagement at a fine-
granularity level of content.
— 6 —

• Contribution 2: The main idea behind this thesis has been im-
plemented in two primary application domains: the evaluation
of code review quality based on code comprehension assessment
and the comprehension and assessment of natural language dif-
ficulties. These implementations are represented by prototype
tools, which are the first in their domains and have a potentially
strong impact as they address two complex problems in the two
domains mentioned.
• Contribution 3: This thesis produced a risk model for compre-
hension issues based on how each content part or region impacts
the whole understanding. This is the first risk assessment of
comprehension difficulties based on biofeedback that the author
is aware of.
• Contribution 4: This work helped open up a new field of study
for the development of a new code complexity meter based on
the perceived difficulty of code comprehension by developers.
Having discussed the goals and the global contributions of this work, the next
section shows the thesis outline and the synthesis of specific contributions in
each chapter.

The story of this thesis and work is told in seven chapters, as follows: Chapter 2:
Thischapterprovidesproofoftheconceptoftheapproach, whichaimstoexplore
the feasibility of utilizing physiological signals derived from cardiac activity and
pupil responses to predict comprehension difficulties within specific regions of
digital content with fine-grained accuracy. Figure 1.2 shows an abstract view of
the approach.
Figure 1.2: TellBack Representation.
In today’s digital age, the way we consume information has shifted dramatic-
ally toward digital resources, with handheld devices like tablets, e-readers, and
— 7 —

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
smartphones becoming our primary reading platforms [Dong et al., 2020]. How-
ever, on-screen reading is associated with shorter attention spans and poorer
comprehension compared to traditional print media. [Jeong and Gweon, 2021].
This shift towards digital resources in different fields and industries has made
the comprehension of digital content a critical area of research, especially as we
increasingly rely on digital materials for learning, training, and work.
The arrival of Industry 4.0 in our lives is transforming the way we operate in tra-
ditional settings. Previously, research efforts focused on improving the physical
comfort and safety of people at work. But as jobs become more complicated,
we are observing that people in a variety of professions, including learners and
employees, also have to cope with cognitive demands and mental stress. Accord-
ing to [Lagomarsino et al., 2022], these mental health issues can have an impact
on employee performance and overall productivity, which is why the Industry
5.0 revolution calls for greater attention to people and their interactions with
machines [Akundi et al., 2022].
For example, code comprehension and code review are common mental tasks in
disciplines like software engineering. In software engineering, human interaction
(in the common sense, as when you play a video game) is not high. However,
the intellectual level of the task creates a specific type of intellectual and cognit-
ive interaction. For example, code review necessitates a deep interaction with
and comprehension of the code and the documentation that goes with it. Our
cognitive load tends to rise when we concentrate on these digital resources (i.e.,
software code) because we must integrate and assess information from several
representations, which makes comprehension a challenging and complex process
[Brüggemann et al., 2023].
Comprehension involves multiple stages of cognition, which we hypothesize can
be captured with non-intrusive biofeedback devices like bracelets, wristbands,
and smartwatches, along with other behavioral data (such as reading patterns).
Using physiological expressions of the Autonomous Nervous System (ANS),
these devices capture the user’s cognitive state and provide feedback accord-
ingly. The work reported in [Al Osman et al., 2014] refers to this procedure as
biofeedback.
This thesis introduces a novel technique called ”Intelligent Biofeedback-
Augmented Content Comprehension” or ”TellBack.” TellBack aims to pinpoint
specific regions of digital content (we call them “hotspots”) that induce com-
prehension difficulties for individuals. Unlike existing approaches that assess
the global cognitive load, TellBack focuses on identifying when and where these
comprehension difficulties occur within the content with high granularity (i.e.,
lines of content). Identifying ’hotspots’ opens the door to providing real-time
assistance to users, helping them overcome comprehension difficulties.
TellBack relies on cognitive load as a key indicator to identify challenging re-
gions of digital content. TellBack predicts when and where users have difficulties
understanding specific content hotspots using machine learning algorithms. In
the next section, we provide a snippet of the methodology used. It is important
to note that in this study we used high-precision medical sensors and a high-
— 8 —

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
resolution eye tracker to demonstrate the viability of the approach, although our
ultimate goal is to use consumer wearables and affordable desktop eye trackers
for their practicality in different workspaces and industries. The TellBack’s
contributions that appeared in [Hijazi et al., 2021a] can be summarized as fol-
lows:
• We introduce a new approach that identifies cognitive load and
comprehension difficulties at a local level within content, de-
parting from the global assessments of cognitive load in existing
methods.
• Instead of using eye tracking solely to assess the cognitive load, as
seen in the state-of-the-art, we employ it to precisely determine
where the user is focusing when physiological biomarkers (such
as HR V and pupillometry) indicate a higher cognitive load.
• Through extensive evaluation, our approach demonstrates signi-
ficantly improved precision and recall compared to recent related
works, outperforming them by 23% and 17%, respectively.
• We address the limitations of single-modality approaches by fus-
ing data from non-intrusive biosensors, such as HR V and pupil-
lometry.
• Machine learning techniques have been utilized to optimize the
prediction of when and where individuals face comprehension
challenges and low engagement on screen.
The TellBack approach and its evaluation can be found in [Hijazi et al., 2021a]
published in the IEEE Access journal.
In educational or professional environments where the human factor is crucial, it
is ideal to have a reasonably priced cognitive workload assessment and compre-
hension evaluation. Thus, by utilizing the biofeedback technology suggested in
TellBack, the application scope of this thesis is broadened to address the human
element in software engineering, namely in code comprehension and code review
evaluation as the first instantiation.
Asdetailedinourpaper[ Hijazietal. ,2022c], wepresentthisfirstinstantiationof
the TellBack approach in Chapter 3. The principal goal is to utilize biofeedback
technology to evaluate the comprehensibility of code during the software code
review process. This will enable the evaluation of the effectiveness of the code
review process and the quality of the process.
Thesoftwaredevelopmentprocessishighlydependentonhumancognition, mak-
ing it susceptible to cognitive failures that lead to software defects (Tanveer
[2021]). An analysis of industrial data has revealed that a significant portion of
severe software defects, approximately 87%, can be attributed to human cognit-
ive failures, regardless of the software development process (Huang et al.[2022]
and Honda and Yamada[2012]).
Despite the availability of modern tools and extensive research in software reli-
— 9 —

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
ability, software products continue to exhibit high bug densities, often ranging
from 1 to 5 bugs per 1000 lines of code [Shah et al., 2012]. The ever-increasing
size of software further exacerbates this issue, as more lines of code correlate
with a higher number of bugs [Sandhu and Batth, 2021].
One established practice in software development to enhance software quality is
code review, which involves manual inspection of source code to identify defects
(bugs) and improve overall quality [Thongtanunam et al., 2022].
Modern code reviews have evolved into more lightweight and informal processes,
often relying on individual reviewers and their skills rather than structured in-
spection groups [Sadowski et al., 2018]. Reviewers may face challenges such as
distractions, fatigue, and, notably, difficulties in comprehending the code under
review[Al-Saiyd,2017], whichcanresultinmissedbugsorfalsepositives. There-
fore, there is a pressing need to improve the quality of code reviews, particularly
in terms of bug-finding effectiveness [Huang et al., 2022].
This implementation of TellBack addresses this critical issue by monitoring and
evaluating the performance of code reviewers at the granular code line level,
assessing the overall quality of code reviews, and pinpointing code regions that
may not have been well reviewed. The use of nonintrusive biosensors, along
with eye tracking, enables the measurement of the reviewer’s engagement and
comprehension levels. The goal is to provide code reviewers with immediate
feedback on code regions that require further attention and to improve bug-
finding effectiveness in code reviews.
The relevance of this instantiation of the TellBack approach lies in its poten-
tial to significantly improve the quality of code reviews, with implications for
software reliability and quality. By addressing human cognitive limitations and
providing targeted guidance to reviewers, the proposed approach has the po-
tential to reduce the prevalence of undetected bugs in real software. Moreover,
it aligns with the evolving landscape of software development, where rapid re-
lease cycles and increasing code complexity demand effective and accurate code
review practices.
The work was published in [Hijazi et al., 2022c], which presented the following
contributions:
• Contribution 1: Proposes a novel method for assessing the qual-
ity of code reviews on an individual basis (i.e., code reviewer)
that makes use of physiological measurements and explainable
machine learning algorithms.
• Contribution 2: Provides empirical findings from a controlled
experiment that employed real code reviewers, genuine code,
realistic bugs, and equipment to collect the physiological signals
needed to surrogate the cognitive challenges of the reviewer.
• Contribution 3: Uses the knowledge domain and data-driven
models, two classification models, to assess the approach’s ac-
curacy, precision, and recall.
— 10 —

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
• Contribution 4: Goes beyond state-of-the-art task-level analysis
by analyzing the cognitive burden of the code reviewers at the
code region level.
These contributions were published in the IEEE Transactions on Software En-
gineering, a leading journal in the field of software engineering.
After the successful validation of using biofeedback technology and sensors to
evaluate the quality of the code review process, the subsequent phase involved
examining the application of affordable desktop eye tracking in conjunction with
wearable sensors and commercial smartwatches.
The aim was to create a functional prototype tool that could be assessed in
real software development environments. Chapter 4 of this thesis reports on the
components, architecture, andevaluationofthistool, whichwenamed”iReview”
[Hijazi et al., 2021b].
The use of these increasingly accurate wearable devices has become de facto for
tracking changes in cognitive load by capturing physiological measures such as
PPG-based heart rate and EDA. This is due to the industry’s rapid and ongo-
ing advancements in wearable technology, including commercial smartwatches,
bracelets, and wristbands, as well as today’s sophisticated algorithms for signal
processing and machine learning [Gjoreski et al., 2021].
Access to reasonably priced desktop eye trackers that could record eye gaze data,
estimate cognitive workload, and, more importantly, correctly identify areas of
the screen that the user is looking at also made it possible to design a tool
prototype that fulfills the vision of TellBack.
The work in [Stojmenova et al., 2018] conducted a comprehensive analysis to
determine, for instance, whether affordable eye-trackers can reliably measure
drivers’ cognitive load. The findings of the authors validate the device’s useful-
ness when used properly.
This work, an instantiation of TellBack, focuses on creating a prototype tool to
assess code review quality based on code comprehension estimations, given the
benefits of the biofeedback and wearable technology indicated above. The sur-
rogate cognitive load of the code developer is measured during the code review to
estimate the level of engagement with the review task and the comprehensibility
of the code.
A developer can carry on focusing, being competent, and giving the task at hand
their full attention. However, the same developer can become stressed, tired,
or distracted for a variety of reasons, which puts the quality of their work at
risk.
Thus, during code development, code reviews, testing, and all other development
processes, an objective estimation of the developer’s cognitive state is a crucial
quality factor that has become more necessary these days due to the growing
distractions and interruptions in our work environments.
Therefore, this work [Hijazi et al., 2021b] presents the following contributions

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
to address the challenges:
• Contribution 1: Present a prototype for a new intelligent tool
prototype that evaluates the quality of code reviews based on
cognitive load and code comprehension assessment.
• Contribution 2: Offer the code review quality at the code region
level, together with a description of the prediction of bad reviews
and a pointer to the poorly reviewed codes.
• Contribution 3: Demonstrate how commercial wristband wear-

The evaluation of this prototype tool was carried out, and the findings were
published as a tool article at the leading software reliability conference (ISSRE)
[Hijazi et al., 2021b]. Additionally, this tool was showcased at other events,
including Portugal’s JNation Day and Germany’s Hannover Messe technology
fair.
As observed from the description above, code comprehension and code review,
as fundamental intellectual software engineering tasks, have been covered. We
used these two software engineering tasks because of their huge importance in
the field. The selection of two touch and high abstract tasks, like code com-
prehension and code review is a challenge that is relevant and vital in various
use cases. Looking at a recent mapping study with 40 years of experience in
the design of code comprehension experiments would tell more [Wyrich et al.,
2023].
Apart from software engineering, in Chapter 5, we extended our application
area to address general content comprehension challenges due to the encouraging
outcomes of utilizing wearable devices, affordable eye-trackers, and lightweight
machine learning algorithms to predict comprehension difficulties in code.
The second use of the TellBack approach, the prediction of natural language
comprehension difficulties, is detailed in Chapter 5. The goal of this instanti-
ation is to assess the cognitive load and comprehension difficulties of natural
languages. In contrast to conventional methods, this application makes use
of wearable technology and eye-tracking data to understand and evaluate hu-
man behavior as well as linguistic understandability at the local level of con-
tent, including paragraphs. Physiological measures are used to achieve this
non-invasively, giving insight into the mental processes involved in reading com-
prehension in digital content.
Digital content conveys abstract concepts through natural languages, particu-
larly in written texts. This complexity is amplified by digital content accessible
via smartphones, tablets, and PCs. [Shatunova et al., 2021] observed a shift
towards digital literacy, albeit with insufficient reading literacy. Reading com-
prehension in digital media is common but complex, involving various mental
and motor processes. Assessing comprehension is challenging, as it extends from
— 12 —

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
individual words to larger linguistic units [Mézière et al., 2021]. [LaBerge and
Samuels, 1974] linked comprehension complexity to thinking in general, high-
lighting the difficulty of comprehension assessment.
Studies such as [Tao and Gao, 2022] reveal the growing use of online content
for language learning, where readers can face lexical and structural complexities.
They revisit challenging sections or use external resources, disrupting their read-
ing flow. [Ji et al., 2022] state that engagement in language learning involves be-
havioral, cognitive, and emotional dimensions, with active participation, effort,
and positive affect playing pivotal roles. However, it remains unclear whether
this framework adequately captures engagement in today’s technology-driven
learning environments, particularly for online language learning.
Failing in content comprehension can lead to anxiety [Beals, 2022], slower rates
of learning and vocabulary growth [Holliman et al., 2016], lower performance
[Wesierski and Jezierska, 2018], and even odd behavior [Dehaney, 2000].
Thus, we propose an innovative intelligent approach that predicts and identi-
fies comprehension difficulties in natural language digital content at fine-grained
local levels (i.e., small paragraphs), offering real-time help potential as future de-
velopment. Our study evaluates language comprehension at the paragraph level
using machine learning and physiological data (Pulse Rate Variability, HRV,
and Electrodermal Activity, EDA) from wearables like smartwatches and wrist-
bands. A synchronized desktop eye tracker contributes to locating hotspots in
the content associated with high cognitive load.
This part of the thesis aims to answer these research questions:
• RQ1) Can PR V/HR V and EDA detect cognitive load changes
during English reading tasks?
• RQ2) Can these biomarkers, combined with an eye tracker,
identify text regions linked to comprehension difficulties?
• RQ3) What is the optimal machine learning model for general-
izing user behaviors in content comprehension?
We conducted a controlled experiment with 40 participants from Portuguese
institutions, providing English texts of varying complexity. Participants wore
Empatica E4 ([Empatica, nd]) wristbands for physiological data and used Tobii
5L desktop eye trackers [Tobii, 2024]. While prior research primarily used single
modalities like eye-tracking or EEG/fMRI for comprehension assessment, prac-
tical constraints limit their real-world use. Our approach, employing lightweight
sensors and wearables, offers a practical alternative. Thus, our contributions to
the field, which are presented in [Hijazi et al., 2024], can be summarized as
follows:
• Contribution 1: Innovative Integration of Wearables and Low-
Cost Eye-Tracking: This work, up to our knowledge, is the
first to combine wearable sensors and an affordable desktop eye-
tracker to predict comprehension difficulties at the paragraph
level within general and natural language content.
— 13 —

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
• Contribution 2: Expanding the Comprehension Assessment
Granularity: The study goes beyond traditional comprehension
assessments, introducing an innovative approach that predicts
comprehension challenges in specific content regions (hotspots).
It integrates the analysis of physiological biomarkers with beha-
vioral and eye-gaze data to enhance prediction accuracy.
• Contribution 3: The work introduces an evaluation setup that
closely simulates a practical prototype, demonstrating its poten-
tial utility in educational and learning applications.
The evaluation results have been reported in a paper and accepted in the Q1
Journal of Scientific Reports (Springer Nature).
Following this work, we extended our efforts to present and evaluate a proto-
type tool that we called ”iMind,” based on the concept of natural language
comprehension difficulties prediction and assessment. The iMind prototype tool
is represented in a web extension for practical use. This work is presented in
Chapter 6, which provides an overview of the tool architecture, components,
and machine-learning modeling flavors. Importantly, this work introduces a
novel scoring method for the risk associated with not comprehending a part of
the content (e.g., a paragraph). In other words, the risk model proposed in this
work aims to assess the impact and consequences of comprehension difficulties
in specific parts of a given text. It combines both quantitative and qualitative
measures to evaluate the risk score represented by the likelihood of comprehen-
sion difficulties, captured by physiological and non-physiological features, and
the impact, measured using measures such as cosine similarity and subjective as-
sessment by experts and AI language models. The overall risk score is computed
based on the likelihood and impact, and a correction factor is applied to account
for the user’s skill level in the language. The risk score is then classified as high
or low risk based on a predefined threshold, providing important information for
future appropriate support interventions.
The work in Chapter 6 presents the following contributions to the field of natural
language understanding assessment.
• Contribution 1: A new prototype tool for comprehension pre-
diction: This work, up to our knowledge, is the first attempt
to develop a prototype web extension tool that predicts natural
language comprehension difficulties at the local level of the con-
tent.
• Contribution 2: Risk assessment in text comprehension: The
research introduces a new method for assessing and scoring the
risk associated with not comprehending parts of content based on
physiological signals, user behavior, and semantic characteristics
of the text.
• Contribution 3: The work includes a machine learning pipeline
that provides different mechanisms for quantifying comprehen-
sion difficulties through the labeling process and presents the

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
evaluation results, including accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-
score in addition to examining the significance of the results
obtained using permutation classification score.
The paper included in Chapter 6 was submitted to a Q1 journal and, at the
time of writing this thesis, is under review.
The thesis is concluded in Chapter 7, which provides a summary of the major
findings from each chapter. It also discusses key challenges and suggests possible

1.4 Ongoing and Emerging Research Activities
The techniques developed in this thesis have initiated a new area of emerging
research. The ongoing research aims to use the code comprehension assessment
methods described in this thesis, with additional modalities, to evaluate code
complexity metrics against human perceptions of difficulty. Thus, the author of
this thesis is cooperating with a Ph.D. student in developing this emerging work.
This shows how objective comprehension assessment using affordable technolo-
gies like the ones utilized and developed in this work can be instrumental in
various domains where intellectually and mentally demanding tasks are domin-
ant.
Welabelthisnewavenueofresearch”emerging”becauseevenwiththeenormous
amount of research, it is still completely unsolved how to predict or measure
software complexity in a way that truly captures the intrinsic complexity of
software artifacts as understood by (human) software programmers. However,
the measure that matters most in the development and maintenance of reliable
software is how complex the code appears to humans. This is due to the fact that
factors like software testability, modifiability, and reusability must be handled
correctly. However, the actual indicator of how difficult it is for humans to
understand code is how difficult it is to handle sufficiently significant factors
like software testability, maintainability, and reusability in order to design and
maintain dependable software [Hao et al., 2023].
This work, [Hao et al.,2023], where the author of this thesis is the second author,
used electroencephalography (EEG) as a reference to assess the cognitive load
of programmers during code comprehension. It makes use of this reference to
assess various viewpoints and approaches to code complexity measurement via a
carefully controlled experiment. A group of twenty-seven software programmers
(B.Sc. and M.Sc. students as well as professional programmers) were asked to
understand a set of programs, the difficulty of which is measured using a variety
of techniques, including code constructions and human-centered approaches. In
particular, this work employed the following techniques to assess and provide
guidelines for human-centric code complexity:
• Traditional code metrics, including:
– Halstead Metrics: These metrics are derived from the number of
— 15 —

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
operators and operands in the code and include measures such as
difficulty, effort, and volume.
– V(g) - Cyclomatic Complexity : This metric measures the com-
plexity of a program by counting the number of linearly independent
paths through the program’s source code.
– LoC - Lines of Code : This is a simple measure of the size of a
program by counting the number of lines in the source code.
• Metrics of cognitive difficulty derived from code structures with scores.
• Cognitive complexity metrics from cutting-edge tools like SonarSource.
• Using eye tracking to directly evaluate programmers’ behavioral patterns
(such as reading duration and revisits).
• Using EEG, which has been suggested as a reference to quantify cognitive
complexity in code comprehension scenarios, the cognitive load of pro-
grammers during code comprehension is directly assessed [Medeiros et al.,
2024].
Additional subjective-oriented techniques based on the NASA-TLX survey were
employed in the experiment to estimate the subjective perceptions of the code
complexity of the participants. The results supported our hypothesis that the
cognitive load measured by EEG in our experiment represents well the difficulty
programmers may feel in comprehending code and constitutes a good yardstick
for comparing complexity metrics. Programmers’ cognitive load was measured
using EEG correlates (rs = 0.829, with p < 0.0001) with the subjective percep-
tion of code complexity assessed by NASA-TLX.
In order to help identify the actual code elements that cause high values of
programmers’ cognitive load, which correspond to code regions that the pro-
grammers perceived as complex, the evaluation results of the various metrics
were discussed at the level of the entire programs used in the code comprehen-
sion tasks (the experiments used three programs), code units (functions) of each
program, and small code regions used in the analysis.
Assessment of metrics such as V (g) and CC-Sonar was a major topic of discus-
sionthroughouttheoutcomediscussionbecauseofitsimportancetothesoftware
industry. More specifically, the work discussed in detail potential causes and po-
tential solutions to improve the accuracy of current code complexity metrics and
presented a comprehensive set of examples in which V(g) and CC-Sonar seemed
to fail in capturing code complexity as perceived by programmers.
The work concludes with a list of recommendations for enhancing current code
complexity measures, especially CC-Sonar, which include both specific recom-
mendations and avenues for further research.
As noted above, in this study, we leverage the experience gained in code com-
prehension assessment as a key to evaluating and constructing a new software
complexity metric based on our findings from earlier work in this thesis.
— 16 —

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
The contributions of this work can be summarized as follows:
• Contribution 1: Presents a novel perspective on software com-
plexity metrics by examining them in the context of perceived
code comprehension.
• Contribution 2: Puts forth guidelines for enhancing current code
complexity metrics, informed by experimental neurophysiolo-
gical data.
• Contribution 3: Conducts a comprehensive comparison of state-
of-the-art complexity metrics in the context of code comprehen-
sion.
This work was published in the Frontiers in Neuroscience journal. Currently, the
work is being expanded to develop a data-driven dynamic metric that can cap-
turetheactualdifficultyofcodecomprehensionperceivedbyprogrammers.

ABSTRACT Assessing comprehension difﬁculties requires the ability to assess cognitive load. Changes
in cognitive load induced by comprehension difﬁculties could be detected with an adequate time reso-
lution using different biofeedback measures (e.g., changes in the pupil diameter). However, identifying
the Spatio-temporal sources of content comprehension difﬁculties (i.e., when, and where exactly the
difﬁculty occurs in content regions) with a ﬁne granularity is a big challenge that has not been explicitly
addressed in the state-of-the-art. This paper proposes and evaluates an innovative approach named Intelligent
BiofeedbackAugmented Content Comprehension (TellBack) to explicitly address this challenge. The goal
is to autonomously identify regions of digital content that cause user’s comprehension difﬁculty, opening the
possibility to provide real-time comprehension support to users. TellBack is based on assessing the cognitive
load associated with content comprehension through non-intrusive cheap biofeedback devices that acquire
measures such as pupil response or Heart Rate V ariability (HRV). To identify when exactly the difﬁculty in
comprehension occurs, physiological manifestations of the Autonomic Nervous System (ANS) such as the
pupil diameter variability and the modulation of HRV are exploited, whereas the ﬁne spatial resolution (i.e.,
the region of content where the user is looking at) is provided by eye-tracking. The evaluation results of this
approach show an accuracy of 83.00% ± 0.75 in classifying regions of content as difﬁcult or not difﬁcult
using Support V ector Machine (SVM), and precision, recall, and micro F1-score of 0.89, 0.79, and 0.83,
respectively. Results obtained with 4 other classiﬁers, namely Random Forest, k-nearest neighbor, Decision
Tree, and Gaussian Naive Bayes, showed a slightly lower precision. TellBack outperforms the state-of-the-art
in precision & recall by 23% and 17% respectively.
INDEX TERMS Biomedical measurement, cognitive load, content comprehension, eye-tracking, heart rate
variability, machine learning.
I. INTRODUCTION
Imagine a software technology that augments your ability to
comprehend complex concepts and ideas. Y ou are reading a
sentence in a technical document and the software installed
in your tablet, laptop, or smartphone automatically detects
speciﬁc passages or words in a paragraph that make the
entire paragraph cumbersome for you and promptly displays
an explanation, shows an example, or provides you with a
deﬁnition that will make you understand the whole idea.
We call this approach InTelligent BiofeedBack Augmented
The associate editor coordinating the review of this manuscript and
approving it for publication was Giuseppe Desolda
 .
Content Comprehension (TellBack), and the present paper
proposes the idea and evaluates the accuracy of TellBack
in the identiﬁcation of speciﬁc content regions (e.g., line,
expression, etc.) that are considered difﬁcult to understand
by the user. The accurate identiﬁcation of content locations
that cause user’s comprehension difﬁculties is a crucial step
to show the feasibility of the proposed approach.
TellBack uses cognitive load as a key element to identify
speciﬁc parts of digital content that cause comprehension
difﬁculty. The goal is to predict that the user is needing
support in understanding the content element/passage that
is causing comprehension difﬁculties while reading digital
content.

H. Hijazi et al.: Intelligent Biofeedback Augmented Content Comprehension (TellBack)
Digitized reading resources are immensely growing. Due
to the popularity of handheld portable devices, such as
e-readers and tablets, reading is becoming more on-screen
than on printed material. However, various studies, such
as [1], showed that on-screen reading is associated with short
attention and poor comprehension. Comprehension of digital
resources deserves more research attention, especially with
the ever-growing reliance on digital resources comprehen-
sion in learning, training, and industry. For instance, in the
software industry, code review is one of the essential tasks
in software engineering to ensure the quality of the delivered
service. Code review requires full code comprehension by the
reviewer. The code and its description are generally digitized
resources that require the reviewer to immensely read and
comprehend.
Digital resources comprehension requires the ability of
users or learners to integrate and evaluate information across
different types of representations. The integration and evalua-
tion are often mentally demanding tasks, which impose extra
cognitive load on learners.
Comprehension, in general, is a higher cognitive process
that imposes a cognitive load and involves different cog-
nitive states, which we hypothesize in TellBack that these
states can be accurately captured by non-intrusive biofeed-
back devices through physiological manifestations of the
Autonomous Nervous Systems (ANS). In the context of this
paper, biofeedback is deﬁned as the process of providing
task-relevant feedback to the user based on her/his cognitive
state (captured through physiological ANS reactions) using
commercially available wearables such as bracelets, watches,
and rings.
Our main contributions in this work can be summarized by
the following points:
• The paper introduces a new technique to detect the cog-
nitive load and thereby the comprehension difﬁculties at
elemental parts of the content (e.g., lines, expressions,
etc.) and not a global assessment of cognitive load as
investigated in the state-of-the-art.
• The use of eye-tracking is not to assess the cognitive load
as it is generally used in the state-of-the-art (which has
the disadvantage of a slow response time) but to provide
an accurate spatial resolution where the user is looking
at when the HRV and pupillometry biomarkers indicate
peaks in the cognitive load.
• The evaluation of the improvements in precision and
recall achieved with TellBack, when compared with a
very recent related study in [12], shows 23% better
precision and 17% better recall.
• This work addresses the limitations of a single modal-
ity using data fusion of non-intrusive biosensors and
real-time biomarkers from HRV and pupillometry.
Unlike other recent studies [2], [3], [12] that exploit the
multimodality of biosensors, we used simple features (in
terms of computation), but discriminant enough to detect
the increase of the user’s cognitive load/mental effort in
real-time.
• The use of machine learning and AI techniques to opti-
mize the prediction of ‘‘when and where’’ the user is
encountering difﬁculty on screen.
The ﬁnal goal of Tellback is to augment the user’s ability to
comprehend complex concepts and ideas through a new type
of intelligent interface that seems to guess when and where
the user is making an unusual effort to grasp the meaning and
to provide the user with accurate contextual help. This would
simplify the life of readers, learners, trainees, and many
others, and would potentially accelerate the learning curve
of intellectual skills. Providing such functionality involves
building an intelligent predictive model that can take human
biomarkers as input, process those biomarkers, and provide a
Spatio-temporal indicator of comprehension difﬁculty as an
output.
Biofeedback devices have been extensively used in
research to study the cognitive process involved in various
activities such as software development (e.g., see a recent
survey on measuring the cognitive load of software develop-
ers [9]). Recent work in the context of programmer’s errors
in software development [4], [5], [6] showed that the inte-
gration of HRV , pupillometry, and eye-tracking allows the
identiﬁcation of the code lines that correspond to an increase
of cognitive load of individual programmers. Although the
goal of the research published in [4], [5], [6] is the annotation
of source code for the prediction of programmers’ errors,
it shows that it is possible to associate programmers’ mental
load in real-time to speciﬁc lines of code or lexical tokens [6].
This has motivated us to explore the same concept in the
much broader context of general content comprehension and
intelligent user interfaces, as proposed in TellBack.
The evaluation presented in this paper is intended to pro-
vide a ﬁrst answer to the following question: How accurate
and precise are biomarkers extracted from HRV , pupil-
lometry, and eye-tracking in detecting, in real-time, where
exactly the content comprehension difﬁculties occur on
screen?
Detecting cognitive load in real-time using multimodal
sources of biosensors has recently emerged as one of
the promising approaches in adaptive and cognition-aware
e-learning [2], [3], [12], [13]. Those very recent studies
were mainly focusing on providing feedback on learners’
engagement and cognitive load changes. However, the idea of
TellBack is to localize the content (either in learning context
or other real-life contexts) to be able to provide contextual
help promptly.
The structure of this paper is as follows: the second section
discusses the background concepts and the related work. The
third section introduces the proposed approach and the meth-
ods that were applied. The fourth section addresses the dataset
and the experimental protocol that was followed. Results
and discussions are explored in the ﬁfth section. Whereas
the sixth section discusses the limitations and threats to the
validity of this approach. Finally, the conclusion and the
future directions are presented in the seventh section of this
paper.

II. BACKGROUND AND RELATED WORK
This section provides background information related to the
biometric measures that were exploited in this work and
provides an overview of the related work. Section II-A brieﬂy
introduces HRV and its use in cognitive load detection and
assessment. Section II-B concisely presents pupillography
and eye-tracking and their use in cognitive load assessment.
Section II-C discusses the related work.
A. HEART RATE VARIABILITY (HRV)
Changes in the cognitive load inﬂuence the way the ANS
regulates the cardiovascular system and causes detectable
variations of the heart rate known as HRV (Heart Rate V ari-
ability). HRV is modulated by the two components of the
ANS (i.e., the sympathetic and the parasympathetic nervous
systems) and it is based on calculating the changes of periods
between consecutive cardiac cycles. HRV can be assessed in
both the time and the frequency domain. In the frequency
domain, for example, the Low Frequency (LF) variability of
the heart rate is associated with the blood pressure control
(i.e., sympathetic), whereas High Frequency (HF) of the heart
rate variability is associated with respiratory sinus arrhythmia
(i.e., parasympathetic).
The idea of using HRV to detect changes in the cognitive
load is not new [21], [29]. However, as indicated in [22],
the within-subject measurements of HRV are still uncertain
because each subject exhibits distinct HRV rhythms. Thus,
non-linear methods of HRV analysis would be prominent
in unveiling the complexity of the HRV rhythms. HRV is
also shown to be sensitive to many factors such as gender,
circadian rhythm, age, prior activities of the subjects, and
breathing conditions. Although these factors may change
from individual to individual, this is not a problem for the use
of HRV in TellBack. We use LF/HF ratio means and spikes
to detect peaks in the cognitive load of speciﬁc individuals,
and thus between-subject measurements are not relevant for
TellBack.
B. PUPILLOGRAPHY AND EYE-TRACKING
Eye pupil response has been recognized as an indica-
tor of cognitive and attentional efforts. V arious research
attempts such as [23], [24] established the evident relation-
ship between pupil activity and attentional cognitive efforts.
Beatty described in [23] that when a person recalls some-
thing from memory or attempts to parse sentences, the pupil
dilates slightly and returns to its normal size after the task is
done. This reaction was called task-evoked pupillary response
(TEPR) [25]. The spectral analysis of the pupil diameter (PD)
is considered a good index for both the mental efforts and
fatigue state.
Eye-tracking devices have also been used in research to
study the eye gaze during distinct mental tasks such as code
comprehension [4], [5], [6]. In principle, eye-tracking is the
process of tracking the eye movement and determining where
the user is looking at or the absolute point of gaze (POG),
which refers to the point in the visual scene at which the user’s
gaze is focused on. Some technologies like in [7] and [8]
used eye gaze to pinpoint difﬁculties in content. For example,
the iDict technology which is described in [8] employs the eye
gaze features to assist nonnative English users who encounter
difﬁculties in interpreting English words. The assistive appli-
cation accordingly translates that word based on the eye
movements. In TellBack the eye-tracker is used to localize the
regional/elemental parts of content that might have caused the
comprehension difﬁculties.
C. RELATED WORK
Table 1 below includes a related work comparison. How-
ever, very few research attempts have been conducted to
address the problem of prediction of speciﬁc passages in
texts that could be difﬁcult to comprehend by users. An early
attempt was made by Sibert and Jacob [7]. These authors
developed a gaze movements-based assistant that delivers
visual and auditory prompting controls to help recognition
and pronunciation of words for remedial reading instruction
whenever a disabled reader encounters a difﬁculty. Later,
Hyrskykari introduced iDict [8] which analyzes eye gaze to
predict difﬁculties in English words for nonnative speak-
ers and accordingly translate or pronounce difﬁcult words.
Those related works [7], [8] lack the use of the information
sources multimodality (i.e., including different biomarkers).
Moreover, they lack the use of machine learning techniques
to predict the difﬁculty. Due to the non-linearity of cog-
nitive load measurements, the use of statistical analysis is
not enough. In TellBack, we utilize different AI techniques
such as feature-level fusion, ensemble decisions, and various
classiﬁcation models.
Table 1 (below) presents state-of-the-art works that used
multimodal biosensors in assessing cognitive load. Some of
these works were mentioned in a mapping study [9] that
provided a thematic analysis of measuring the cognitive load
of software developers while performing mental tasks. The
study included 33 articles from 11 search engines.
The authors in [9] showed that 55% of the studies used
EEG in monitoring the cognitive load, 36% used a combina-
tion of sensors, 6% used eye-tracking, and 3% used fMRI.
The recent works started to investigate the multimodality of
the input sources in different scopes. Table 1 (above) summa-
rizes those studies and other studies to show the biomarkers
that were used to assess the cognitive load, the methodology,
the results achieved, and the limitations.
Since the idea of TellBack is new, it was not easy to perform
a meaningful comparison between TellBack and the previous
studies. Nevertheless, Table 1 brieﬂy discusses the limitations
of the previous works in a possible utilization scenario of
identiﬁcation of user’s content comprehension difﬁculties.
As noticed, most of the studies that used multimodal input
sensors address software development, code comprehension,
and e-learning scenarios. There are very few studies that
addressed other scopes and other types of content with the
limitation of a single modality to assess the cognitive load.

H. Hijazi et al.: Intelligent Biofeedback Augmented Content Comprehension (TellBack)
For example, [10] used EEG to assess the cognitive load of
users while interpreting different representations of data visu-
alizations (i.e., data distributions). Another example, pupil
dilation was used in [11] as a metric to assess linguistics
representations comprehension.
In summary, the studies that have introduced the idea
of detecting the Spatio-temporal sources of comprehension
difﬁculty in digital content lack the fusion from multimodal
biosensor as well as the generality of content being addressed
in comprehension difﬁculty. The relevant study in [12] used
the multimodal sources of information to detect the mind
wandering moments of readers and not their comprehension
difﬁculties. The studies that used the multimodality model
of sensors such as [13], [14], [15], [17] were in general sup-
porting software development and narrow speciﬁc domains.
Even in other domains, there was no such ability to localize
the content that caused the comprehension difﬁculty at a very
ﬁne granularity level.
Another evident aspect is that the use of advanced AI
techniques is not commonly used in such proposals. For
example, and back to the mapping study in [9], 40% of the
studies that assessed the cognitive load of software developers
do not use any sort of machine learning techniques, whereas
the rest are limited by using only classiﬁcation techniques for
different sets of biosensing features. Most used classiﬁcation
techniques are (according to [9]) Support V ector Machine
(SVM) 15%, Naive Bayes 15%; Multi-algorithms for clas-
siﬁcation 9%; K-means 3%; Decision Tree 3%; Logistic
Regression 3%; Neural Network 3%; Random Forest 3%;
Linear Regression 3%, and Relevance V ector 3%.
To measure the cognitive load associated with mental
efforts (e.g., content comprehension), we must distinguish
between the objectivity (subjective vs objective) methods
and the causality (direct vs indirect) methods. In our evalu-
ation experiments, the subjective perception of the cognitive
load was assessed using self-reporting through the NASA-
TLX [18] questionnaire, whereas objective measurement was
obtained using peripheral physiological responses driven by
the autonomic nervous system (ANS) captured by biofeed-
back low-intrusive sensors.
In TellBack we propose using HRV and eye-tracking
(including pupillography) since there are multiple low- intru-
sive solutions to implement these sensors. A total of 83% of
the explored studies used eye-tracker and eye-related features
(e.g., blink), 66% used skin conductance or EDA measures,
and 50% included EEG. The comparison with TellBack was
not easy because the ideas presented were not similar. How-
ever, we used [12] as a good example of a related recent study
to compare TellBack with. The precision and Recall were the
valid measures to perform the comparison which is shown
in Table1.
III. METHODOLOGY
As described earlier, TellBack is a new idea to automati-
cally identify a user’s difﬁculties in comprehending speciﬁc
passages/elements during the reading of contents on tablets,
laptops, or smartphones. Our vision for TellBack is a future
intelligent and attentive user-interface [16] for enhancing
comprehension using, for example, pop-ups containing rel-
evant information on the topic extracted from the web could.
People could indeed stop reading when encountering a com-
prehension difﬁculty and could google the meaning of the
obscure elements in the content. However, in practice, this
rarely happens, due to the rush of the moment or due to the
interaction limitations of small devices such as smartphones,
which is the de facto computational platform in both the
developed and developing world. Therefore, the key message
is often lost because of these unsolved difﬁculties, which hin-
ders proper decision making and leads to all sorts of mistakes,
drastically disturbing the learning process and having a huge
negative impact on society.
Figure 1 below shows a schematic representation of Tell-
Back main components. This representation can be divided
into three distinct phases: bio-signals acquisition, signal pro-
cessing, machine learning models, and content annotation
based on comprehension difﬁculty classiﬁcation.
Recent results appeared in [6] in the context of assessing
cognitive state (mainly high cognitive load and distraction
states) of software programmers show that it is possible to
associate such cognitive information, in real-time, to speciﬁc
lines of code or lexical tokens, to identify such code lines
as being more susceptible of having software bugs. In the
work reported herein, we will expand such preliminary results
obtained in the speciﬁc context of prevention of software
bugs, to a much broader context of general content compre-
hension. The approach followed in TellBack can be summa-
rized in the following components as shown in Figure 1:
1) Real-time ANS biomarkers measurements (HRV ,
Pupillography).
2) Features extraction and selection to assess cognitive
load spikes and patterns.
3) Machine learning techniques to improve detection of
high mental effort/cognitive load from the extracted
features.
4) Eye-tracker to localize content regions that are associ-
ated with high mental effort.
ANS signals are captured from subjects while performing
content comprehension on screen. The signals used to assess
the cognitive load are sampled with timestamps allowing
the identiﬁcation of the moments when the user is perform-
ing the comprehension task. The signals obtained are fed
into the feature extraction and selection module. In this mod-
ule, the domain knowledge is used to choose the most rele-
vant biomarkers out of the signals. Using those discriminant
features extracted from the collected pupillography and HRV
synchronized with eye-tracker (that will be illustrated in the
next section), elemental digital contents associated with such
features are classiﬁed into two main binary classes, i.e., ‘‘Dif-
ﬁcult’’ and ‘‘Not Difﬁcult’’. Those classes are considered the
comprehension difﬁculty state as shown in Figure 1.
