4. Preprocessing and Feature Engineering198
During the experiment described in the previous section, we collected the following data:199
1. Blood Volume Pulse (BVP):represents an indirect index of heart activity by detecting the200
changes in blood volume.201
2. The inter-beat interval (IBI):represents the time between successive heartbeats and202
serves to estimate heart rate and PRV. Both BVP and IBI data are available in CSV format203
through the Empatica platform [21].204
3. EDA:represents the conductance of the skin, or the galvanic response of the skin. EDA205
has two components; tonic-level EDA, represents the signal’s slower changes. The second206
component, known as phasic EDA, represents the signal’s rapid fluctuations, particularly207
the Skin Conductance Response (SCR). Although 0.05 uS is the typical SCR threshold,208
the experimental setting can cause it to fluctuate between 0.01 and 0.05 uS.209
4. Reading time:represents the total time that the volunteer spent in a given local region of210
the text. Reading time is calculated by summing up all fixation duration in a given part of211
the text. According to some related studies (e.g., [22]), the minimum fixation duration is212

Research Article 8
typically 200 milliseconds, whereas the total time of all fixations should not be less than213
30 seconds to be deemed as a content reading time to ensure capturing the proper response214
of the PRV and the EDA [23].215
5. Number of Revisits:represents the number of times the volunteer regresses their eyes to216
a specific local region of the text.217
6. Local coordinates and timestamp:represents the coordinates at which the volunteer is218
lookingalongwiththetimestampofthefixation, whichwasutilizedinthesynchronization219
process.220
7. Volunteers perception and performance:the perceived difficulty and performance221
of volunteers were represented by NASA-TLX response, the number of red and yellow222
highlights and the number of incorrect answers to the comprehension questions.223
The next step after acquiring these signals and data is feature extraction and selection. The224
feature extraction is based on our domain knowledge of physiological features that can accurately225
surrogate the cognitive load. See previous studies (e.g., [24], [25]). Before presenting the feature226
space, it is worth mentioning that recording scroll movements provides crucial information about227
the position of text regions over time. By synchronizing the scroll data (sampled at 10 Hz)228
with the eye gaze data (sampled at 100 Hz), we could identify visits and revisits to the text229
regions. This synchronization involves updating the Y value of the eye gaze with the scroll value.230
Subsequently, the combined array is downsampled to 5 Hz for faster computational processing.231
The preprocessing of PRV and EDA data involves critical steps to enhance signal quality and232
accuracy. The preprocessing of the PRV involves removing IBI values outside the range of 250233
to 2000 ms and eliminating outliers that differ more than 20% from the previous IBI values [26].234
Furthermore, as shown in Table 2, the frequency domain characteristics are calculated using235
the Lomb-Scargle Periodogram [27], which calculates the power or significance of different236
frequencies in the periodogram. Non-linear features such as signal entropy and the ratio of the237
Poincaré plot axes are also extracted to capture the complexity and balance of heart variability.238
For EDA, the preprocessing includes noise removal and artifact elimination using a 3rd-order239
Butterworth low-pass filter with a cutoff frequency of 0.1 Hz. The EDA signal is decomposed240
intophasicandtoniccomponentsusingaconvexoptimizationapproach[28],andthepeaksofthe241
SkinConductanceResponse(SCR)areidentifiedtoderivephasicfeatures. ThepeaksoftheSCR242
componentareidentifiedusingadynamicthresholdresponsemethod[29]. Thismethodaccounts243
for the collective activity of multiple sweat glands, each with different activation thresholds.244
Peaksaredetectedaslocalmaximathatexceedacertainthreshold,whichisdynamicallyadjusted245
based on the signal characteristics. Finally, eye-tracking features include reading time, and the246
number of revisits were extracted to provide insights into gaze behavior while interacting with247
the content.248
Table 2 shows PRV, EDA, eye movement, and user profile features that were used in the249
model. It is essential to note that we extracted 67 features from all the modalities of the250
physiological responses. However, we used domain knowledge (i.e., handcrafted features), and251
the mutual information (MI) data-driven approach [30] to select the best features that optimize252
the performance of the machine learning models.253
During the feature selection phase, particularly for the handcrafted features, we incorporated254
well-established metrics known to significantly impact text comprehension. These include the255
numberofrevisitstoaspecificpartofthetextandtheuser’slanguageproficiencyandexperience.256
The MI was used to select the best ten features out of the sixty- seven extracted features. The MI257
is a data-driven filter feature selection approach, which is known as computationally efficient258
compared with wrapper methods.259

5. Risk Analysis of Comprehension Difficulties in Text286
Asdiscussedearlierinthispaper,whilereadingnaturallanguagecontentontheWeb,peoplemay287
encounter comprehension difficulties in certain regions or parts of the content (e.g. paragraphs).288
However, assessing the impact of this comprehension difficulty on the overall understanding of289
content is often challenging and difficult to quantify. Imagine a tool that assigns a risk score to290
each part of the content, indicating both the level of comprehension difficulty and its impact on291
overallunderstanding. ThisriskassessmentiswhatweintroducehereasafunctionalityofiMind.292
The overall goal of this risk assessment is to demonstrate the impact and consequences of293
comprehensiondifficultiesinspecificpartswithinagiventext. Theassessmenttakesintoaccount294
both the quantitative and qualitative measures, with the likelihood of comprehension difficulties295
captured by the features we selected (physiological and non-physiological). Figure 7 shows an296
overview of the assessment framework proposed in this work.
Fig. 7. Risk analysis framework of iMind
297
To compute the risk associated with each region of the text, we relied on equation 3, which298
combines the likelihood (or probability) and the impact (or consequence) of a certain event if it299
does occur. In our context, the likelihood is determined by the machine learning classifier at300
the local level of the text. In other words, likelihood is the probability that a certain part of the301
text is difficult to comprehend by an individual based on the physiological and nonphysiological302
features explained earlier.303
Risk = Impact Score×Likelihood (3)
However, the impact represents the consequence of not comprehending a given region of304
the text on the overall or global understanding of the content. To compute the impact in each305
region of the text, we use two measures, namely, quantitative and qualitative measures. The306
quantitative measure is represented by the cosine similarity. The cosine similarity metric has307
seen extensive use in the evaluation of semantic coherence and similarity, particularly with the308
growing popularity of vector space models for text representation [34].309

Research Article 11
In our context, we utilize the cosine similarity metric to assess the semantic similarity between310
paragraphs and the original text, providing a quantitative measure to assess the contribution of311
each paragraph to the overall comprehension of the content. This metric examines the semantic312
similarities and distinctive features of individual paragraphs compared to the entire text, offering313
a numerical basis for understanding the significance of each paragraph within the broader314
comprehensibility of the content. In other words, if a text contains two paragraphs, A and B, the315
goal of using cosine similarity is to evaluate the semantic similarity of each paragraph with the316
entire text. This helps determine which paragraph, A or B, contributes more to understanding the317
overall content of the text.318
We hypothesize that paragraphs with higher cosine similarity to the entire text contain central319
informationthatsignificantlycontributestotheoverallunderstandingofthetext. Thisisbasedon320
the assumption that the paragraphs should not be redundant, and readers typically follow a linear321
path of reading. Incoherent paragraphs, which may cause greater comprehension difficulties as322
noted by [35], often do not contribute significantly to the overall coherent understanding of the323
text. These paragraphs tend to have a low impact on the reader’s overall comprehension. From a324
text question perspective, Zheng et al. [36] analyzed human behavior in reading comprehension.325
Volunteers were asked to read questions before the reading tasks and then seek answers in the326
texts. The authors suggest that texts with higher cosine similarity to a question tend to attract327
more human attention and comprehensibility, as predicted by their average word reading times328
(i.e., high impact). This finding shows the influence of semantic similarity, measured by cosine329
similarity, on the allocation of human attention during reading tasks.330
Initially,eachparagraphandtheentiretextareencodedinto384-dimensionalembeddingsusing331
the transformer model (i.e., paraphrase-MiniLM-L6-v2) [37] to ensure a proper dimensionality332
alignment between different paragraphs and texts. Subsequently, cosine similarity is computed333
between each paragraph and the entire text based on their embedding.334
The cosine similarity between𝑛 paragraphs𝑃1,𝑃 2,...,𝑃 𝑛 and the entire text𝑇 is calculated335
as shown in Equation 3:336
Cosine Similarity(𝑃𝑖,𝑇) = 𝑃𝑖·𝑇
∥𝑃𝑖∥∥𝑇∥ (3)
where𝑃𝑖·𝑇 is the dot product of the vectors representing paragraph𝑃𝑖 and the entire text𝑇,337
and∥𝑃𝑖∥ and∥𝑇∥ are the respective magnitudes of those vectors.338
For a better comparison between paragraphs and texts, we normalize the cosine similarity339
scores to the range [0, 1]. We use the following transformation:340
Normalized Similarity(𝑃𝑖,𝑇) = Cosine Similarity(𝑃𝑖,𝑇)+ 1
2 (4)
The contribution ratio of each paragraph𝑃𝑖 to understanding the full text𝑇 is calculated based341
on the normalized similarities. This is shown in Equation 5:342
Contribution Ratio𝑖 = Normalized Similarity(𝑃𝑖,𝑇)˝𝑛
𝑗=1Normalized Similarity(𝑃 𝑗,𝑇)× 100 (5)
where 𝑛 is the total number of paragraphs. This equation ensures that the sum of the343
contribution ratios of all paragraphs equals 100344
This method provides a clear and quantifiable way to assess the contribution of each paragraph345
to the overall understanding of the text. A higher normalized similarity indicates that the346
paragraphcontainscentralcontentrelativetothefulltext,whichmayimplyagreatercontribution347
to the comprehension of the content.348
On the other hand, the qualitative evaluation of impact is expressed as a percentage value,349
referred to as the "qualitative contribution." This value is derived from a qualitative analysis,350

Research Article 12
incorporating subjective assessments from language experts in English and the Large Language351
Model GPT 3.5. We asked human experts and AI through GPT 3.5 davinci-002 to assign a score352
toeachparagraphbasedonitscontributiontounderstandingthewholecontent. equation6shows353
how we combine each score to have an integrated representation of the qualitative assessment.354
Qualitative Contribution=𝑤Human×Human Score+𝑤AI×AI Score (6)
where𝑤Human and𝑤AI are the weights assigned to human experts and AI, respectively. To355
synthesize the quantitative and qualitative aspects, the impact score is calculated as equation 7356
shows, as follows:357
Impact Score=𝑤1×Cosine Similarity+𝑤2×Qualitative Contribution (7)
where𝑤1 and𝑤2 represent the weights assigned to the quantitative and qualitative measures,358
respectively. It is worth noting that all weights from equations (6) and (7) are learnable weights359
using linear regression based on human experts’ ground-truth scores.360
Since the risk of comprehension difficulties differs across users based on their experience361
and prior knowledge, a user-experience level-based adjustment factor is added to improve the362
risk analysis. The volunteers’ language proficiency level and academic degree determined their363
experience level. Language proficiency level was found to be significantly correlated with both364
academic performance and comprehension. [38].365
As a result, the impact score is adjusted by the correction factor to account for the user’s skill.366
For example, if the user is an "expert," the impact score can be lowered by using a correction367
factor with a range of values from 0-0.99. Put another way, because of this correcting factor,368
the expert will receive a lower impact score than the normal user if two users have the same369
probability of comprehension difficulty in the same text they are reading. The correction factor,370
as shown in equation 8, is applied as follows:371
Corrected Impact Score= Impact Score×Correction Factor (8)
This integrated approach, encompassing quantitative, qualitative, correction for experience,372
and risk assessments, provides a comprehensive understanding of the potential challenges posed373
bycomprehensiondifficulties. Thecorrectedriskscore,denotedby 𝐶𝑅𝑆 isfedintoaquantization374
function called "Risk Classification" to classify the score as high risk or low risk based on a375
predefined threshold, as shown in equation 9.376
Risk Classification(𝐶𝑅𝑆,𝑡ℎ𝑟𝑒𝑠ℎ𝑜𝑙𝑑 ) =
(
low, if𝐶𝑅𝑆 <threshold;
high, if𝐶𝑅𝑆≥ threshold. (9)
The threshold here is a value defined based on the design and application requirements. For377
example, the threshold can be very low in conservative scenarios where the content is very378
sensitive and important to comprehend.379
The risk score demonstration can offer important information on the appropriate support380
intervention. For instance, an intervention aimed at reducing the risk can either lower the381
impact or the likelihood by enhancing the content displayed using LLMs (e.g., GPT). The LLMs382
can summarize the high-risk region of the content, change its position, or simply explain it383
with images and videos. To put the risk analysis results into action, looking at Figure 8, we384
observed interesting behaviors in the users’s engagement with the text. In the first paragraph385
indicated by T1R1 (Text 1, Region 1), despite being a non-expert user at a B1 level in English,386

the dataset features and the corresponding labels.528
Threats to Validity529
Studies involving controlled experiments and detailed analysis of human behavior using complex530
measurements often face various threats to validity.531
One internal threat is the reliance on cosine similarity to assess the contribution of each532
paragraph. This metric assumes that paragraphs with higher similarity to the entire text contain533
central information crucial for understanding the text as a whole. However, this assumption534
may not always hold. For instance, a paragraph might exhibit high cosine similarity with the535
text but contain redundant information not essential for comprehension. To address this, we536

The use of specific wearable devices, such as the Empatica E4 and Tobii 5L eye-tracker,544
presentsanotherintrinsicthreattovalidityduetopotentialissueswithscalabilityandaccessibility.545
Not all working environments or learning settings can afford these devices. To mitigate this, we546

Furthermore, the controlled experimental setting might pose an external threat, potentially548
limiting the generalizability of our findings to real-world reading scenarios. This limitation549
underscores the need for further experiments and validation with more diverse content.550
Lastly, the demographic and educational characteristics of our study’s volunteers might not551
represent the general population’s diversity. This limitation could affect the applicability of552
our findings to individuals with different linguistic backgrounds, cognitive abilities, or reading553
preferences. To mitigate this threat, we are recruiting a more diverse group of volunteers and554
using varied content types.555

— 111 —

CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSIONS, CHALLENGES AND FUTURE WORK
N
ew solutions to support the interaction between humans and machines
have attracted the attention of researchers and engineers as a part of
the industry 5.0 revolution. Therefore, this thesis proposes a novel
approach to assess and predict content comprehension difficulties by leveraging
non-intrusive biosensors and wearables while users are interacting with a given
digital content. This chapter provides a conclusion and discusses the future
directions of this research.
7.1 Conclusions and Implications
In our distracting environments, the rapid development of content, especially
with the exponential growth of AI generative tools, and the complexity of in-
tellectual tasks around us, it is relevant to identify and pinpoint difficulties and
challenges with comprehending digital and online content. Assessing compre-
hension in an accurate time can prevent negative consequences such as, but not
limited to, learning barriers, questionable decision quality, and potentially less
productivity, among many other consequences.
In this thesis, we introduced a novel approach to predicting content comprehen-
sion difficulties at the local level of content that we called ”hotspots” through
the assessment of cognitive load, which can be quantified using physiological sig-
nals such as Heart Rate Variability (HRV), Pulse Rate Variability (PRV), Elec-
trodermal Activity (EDA), and eye-tracking data. These signals are acquired
through non-invasive biosensors and wearables to serve as a reliable indicator of
cognitive load changes during different mental tasks.
Cognitive load changes alone cannot accurately capture the underlying com-
prehension difficulties encountered during mentally demanding tasks, such as
content comprehension. Various factors, including the nature of the task, can
significantly impact performance. Therefore, our research began, in the general
form, with a commonly used comprehension task in the proof of concept phase
(reported in Chapter 2), which is code comprehension. Code comprehension is
a complex task, formal, and provides a rich context for assessing content com-
prehension. Additionally, even with the advent of code-generative tools, code
comprehension holds significant relevance in software engineering, which is one
of the biggest industries.
Cognitive load changes alone cannot accurately capture the un-
derlying comprehension difficulties encountered during mentally
demanding tasks.
Our technique objectively identifies ”when and where” content comprehension
difficulties occur on the screen. This thesis contributes to two main applica-
tion areas providing solutions to two complex problems: software engineering
— 112 —

CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSIONS, CHALLENGES AND FUTURE WORK
(specifically code review evaluation problem) and natural language comprehen-
sion assessment problem, through the development of two models and prototype
tools, iReview and iMind, respectively. In both areas, comprehension is a key
aspect, making our comprehension assessment at local levels (i.e., precise gran-
ularity) a significant contribution.
Our technique objectively identifies “when and where” content
comprehension difficulties occur on the screen.
In our proof of concept phase, we achieved an accuracy of83.00% ± 0.75 in
classifying regions of content as difficult or not difficult using a Support Vector
Machine (SVM). The precision, recall, and micro F1-score are 0.89, 0.79, and
0.83, respectively. we found that including only Heart Rate Variability (HRV)
features led to the best results in terms of accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-
score when using Support Vector Machines (SVM) with the Radial Basis Func-
tion (RBF) kernel. This indicates that HRV alone can effectively explain the
perceived difficulty in understanding code at the code line level. However, due
to the non-specificity of the HRV, other features, including pupil information
and experience level were also fed into the machine learning model to enhance
its specificity.
HR V alone can effectively explain the perceived difficulty in un-
derstanding code at the code line level, but combining multiple
physiological signals enhances the robustness and specificity of
the model.
For example, the SVM with the RBF kernel performed the best when both
pupil and HRV data were integrated. On the contrary, the worst results were
obtained when only pupillography measurements were used. This highlights the
importance of combining multiple physiological signals for better performance
in understanding content comprehension difficulties.
While HRV alone yielded the best results, feature fusion of HRV and Pupillo-
graphy using k-nearest neighbors (KNN) provided the best performance overall.
This suggests that combining different physiological features can enhance the ro-
bustness and specificity of the model using different classification models.
The evaluation revealed that specific HRV and pupillography features, such as
the number of LF/HF ratio spikes and the mean values of LF/HF ratio spikes,
could provide valuable insights into the difficulty experienced by subjects in
understanding code at the code line level and over time.
The contribution of TellBack lies in its unique approach of combining physiolo-
gical measures, contextual information, eye-tracking data, fed into machine
learning techniques to detect comprehension difficulties at local levels of con-
tent.
This success has led to concrete applications in two domains (i.e., software engin-
eering and natural languages). We oriented the first application of the TellBack
— 113 —

CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSIONS, CHALLENGES AND FUTURE WORK
concept to software engineering. We performed a second proof of concept to test
the viability of TellBack in evaluating the effectiveness and quality of code re-
views from the human factor by assessing reviewers’ code comprehension. Mod-
erncode reviews, whichare essentialtoidentifybugsand ensuresoftwarequality,
are often subject to human limitations, such as reviewer distraction, fatigue, and
difficulty fully comprehending the code under review.
However, the quality evaluation of the code review at code region levels requires
the integration of other contextual and code-oriented characteristics, such as
code complexity, to give the final prediction of the code review quality. Fur-
thermore, the model integrates behavioral and personal characteristics, such
as review time, number of visits to the code region under review, and review
experience.
The results show that the approach could predict the review quality with an
accuracy of 87.77% ± 4.65.
The iReview tool presents a novel approach to the evaluation
of code reviews, focusing on local code regions rather than the
entire program, which enhances the accuracy of detecting poorly
reviewed code.
One of the interesting findings shed light on the best features selected for clas-
sification, namely HRV frequency domain peaks mean, HRV frequency domain
peaks quantile, LF/HF ratio, pupil diameter peaks median, HRV time-domain
SDNN and RMSDD, pupil diameter peaks mean, pupil diameter quantile, ex-
pertise, review time, and revisits.
The novelty of this research lies in various aspects, such as its integration of
physiological data, such as Heart Rate Variability and pupillary response, with
AI techniques to estimate a surrogate of the cognitive load of reviewers during
code reviews at local levels of code (e.g., a couple of code lines). This innovative
approach goes beyond traditional code review tools by focusing on individual
reviewer performance and cognitive processes, addressing the limitations of ex-
isting tools.
The final outcome of this approach is a prediction of the quality of code review
at the local level of code (i.e., lines of code), a pointer to the lines or regions of
code that were not well reviewed and covered, and finally an explanation of the
prediction result. This outcome can help programmers and code reviewers make
critical decisions to perform a second pass of review on those regions of code
marked as ”not well reviewed” or invite other reviewers to perform the task. As
this work contributed significantly to the domain of software engineering, it was
published in a leading journal of software engineering.
One of our strategic conclusions is that any successful realization of the approach
requires concrete prototyping. Based on the outcomes of the work presented in
Chapter 3 and the promising performance of biofeedback measures in assessing
the quality of code reviews using lightweight and accurate sensors, we presented
a prototype tool to evaluate the quality of code reviews, which we call ”iReview.”
— 114 —

CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSIONS, CHALLENGES AND FUTURE WORK
For practicability purposes, the prototype tool iReview utilizes wearables such
as wrist bands and smartwatches that are widely used by a wide array of pro-
grammers and software engineers, in addition to affordable desktop eye tracking
devices.
Current code review tools often fall short of assessing the genuine quality of a
reviewer’s work. They neglect to consider the cognitive state of the reviewer,
which is a critical aspect of the process. Furthermore, existing evaluation meth-
ods tend to provide a broad assessment of the entire review task rather than
delving into the specific regions or sections of code being reviewed.
Thus, the iReview tool presents a novel approach to the evaluation of code
reviews, focusing on local code regions rather than the entire program. iReview
focuses on the human factor of the review process by incorporating physiological
and non-physiological features from the reviewer while doing the task. The data
are then fed into an explainable ML classifier; the tool can effectively identify
code regions that have not been reviewed. The accuracy ranged from 75% to
87% in detecting bad reviews (i.e., code regions classified as badly reviewed still
have undetected bugs).
The iReview tool addresses the limitations of existing code re-
view evaluation methods by focusing on local code regions,
providing valuable insights for programmers and reviewers to
enhance code quality.
Beyond code review evaluation, the implications of such an approach can in-
clude broader applications for iReview in educational and training settings. Ob-
serving novice programmers’ behavior during code review and comprehension
can provide valuable feedback to trainers or work superiors, enabling targeted
interventions and improvements. In addition to its current capabilities, the tool
can be further enhanced by training it on the best practices of code review
demonstrated by expert reviewers. This training could incorporate optimal eye
movement patterns, review durations, and revisits to specific code regions with
varying complexities. By integrating these best practices into the tool, new-
comers to the review process can be guided more effectively, leading to improved
code review outcomes.
The second application area and complex problem we addressed is the natural
language comprehension difficulties prediction. To solve this problem, we used
the same techniques of TellBack, while widening the options of feature engin-
eering, models of classification, and performance metrics. In our digital world,
where written and AI-generated content is accessed through various digital plat-
forms, readers encounter complex language structures and lexical nuances that
can hinder comprehension. Traditional methods of assessing comprehension dif-
ficulties may not provide objective insights into users’ cognitive states or identify
specific content regions that pose challenges to comprehension.
Our contribution to the area of natural language understanding lies in the in-
troduction of a novel method for evaluating language comprehension difficulties
— 115 —

CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSIONS, CHALLENGES AND FUTURE WORK
in digital content at a small paragraph level of granularity. The combination
of eye-tracking technology and wearables to capture physiological data, such as
Pulse Rate Variability (PRV) and Electrodermal Activity (EDA), allowed for
the identification of content at the paragraph level of granularity that is linked
to comprehension difficulties and high cognitive load.
In addressing natural language comprehension difficulties, the
work’s innovative method integrates physiological data, eye-
tracking, and machine learning advancing comprehension assess-
ment at the paragraph level.
For the evaluation of this work, a controlled experiment was conducted with 40
participants from higher education and research institutions. The participants
were provided with three English texts of varying complexity and equipped with
wearable devices to capture physiological data, along with an eye-tracker to
monitor eye movements. The participants were asked to read the texts carefully
and answer comprehension questions after each task. By involving a relatively
good number of volunteers in the controlled experiment, we were able to gather
diverse data points and insights into the participants’ cognitive load during
reading comprehension tasks. The acquired data were preprocessed using state-
of-the-art techniques. This approach achieved an accuracy of72.11% ± 2.21, a
precision of 0.77, a recall of 0.70, and an F1 score of 0.73.
In terms of machine learning models, Logistic Regression stood out as the most
effective classifier for the predictive model. Known for its simplicity, robustness
to noise in the input space, and provision of interpretable results, Logistic Re-
gression proved to play a good role in understanding the factors contributing to
comprehension difficulty. The model evaluation process employed Leave-One-
Subject-Out Cross-Validation to mitigate bias and overfitting, ensuring the ro-
bustness and generalizability of the predictive model across different modalities,
feature selection methods, and classifiers.
After the promising performance of this approach in predicting natural language
understanding, we developed the iMind tool, which is a web extension, that
leveraged the techniques used in TellBack’s second instantiation (inChapter
5) with additional advancements in feature engineering and modeling. Import-
antly, we introduced a risk assessment analysis to score each paragraph of the
English text based on the likelihood of comprehension difficulty and the impact
(i.e., consequence) of not comprehending that part of the content on the overall
understanding of the content.
KeyresultsfromthestudydemonstratetheiMindtool’sperformance, withKNN
and Gradient Boosting classifiers achieving a mean accuracy of 0.70, precision
of 0.70, recall of 0.75, and an F1 score of 0.72.
A permutation classification test confirmed the statistical significance of the
model’s performance, rejecting the null hypothesis with p-values below 0.05
for all metrics. Additionally, the risk score model effectively classified content
regions as high or low risk for comprehension difficulties, providing actionable
— 116 —

CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSIONS, CHALLENGES AND FUTURE WORK
insights to enhance content accessibility and understanding. The risk analysis
of the iMind tool is reported inChapter 6
By providing insights into users’ cognitive load during language comprehension
tasks, and correlating the cognitive load changes with the comprehensive level,
the work opens up possibilities for developing intelligent interfaces that offer im-
mediate contextual support to mitigate comprehension difficulties. In general,
the study findings have implications for the design of personalized learning tools
and content adaptation strategies, showing the promising potential of wearable
technologies and intelligent biosensors in the field of comprehension and engage-
ment assessment.
Finally, we conclude that TellBack approach was successfully applied to two
complex problems in two different domains: software engineering, particularly
code review evaluation, and natural language understanding with acceptable
performance and affordable setup.
7.2 Challenges and Best Practices
Despite the promising performance of the iReview and iMind tools, some chal-
lenges were encountered during their design and development. For example,
smartwatches and wristbands, while practical, often lack the specificity and pre-
cision needed to accurately capture cognitive load without contextual informa-
tion. Body movements can confound physiological signals, necessitating careful
preprocessing and analysis.
Additionally, established guidelines for preprocessing wearable data to quantify
comprehension difficulties are lacking. Nevertheless, selecting appropriate ma-
chine learning pipelines (focusing on feature engineering) and precise baseline
estimation can help mitigate these issues. For instance, devices like the Empat-
ica E4 wristband offer high precision, but capturing cognitive load as part of
the cognitive state remains challenging without adequate processing steps and
suitable models.
Desktop eye-trackers also present challenges, as calibration and setup challenges
can limit their widespread adoption, particularly in large industries. Adapt-
ing this technology to learning environments requires more flexible and afford-
able techniques. While gaming and desktop eye-trackers are relatively easy to
use, they still require considerable setup. Exploring alternatives, such as using
computer webcams for eye gaze data, could improve accessibility and scalabil-
ity.
Privacy and ethical concerns are significant when using biofeedback technology.
Individual monitoring and data privacy issues must be carefully managed to
maintain user trust and comply with privacy standards. Regulatory frameworks
and ethical guidelines are essential to ensure responsible deployment and use of
cognitive load evaluation tools, especially in educational settings.
Quantifying language comprehension presents its own set of challenges. Training
machine learning models to predict comprehension difficulties based on physiolo-
— 117 —

CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSIONS, CHALLENGES AND FUTURE WORK
gical data and even other contextual features is complex and requires robust
ground truth data, such as participants’ annotations and responses to compre-
hension questions.
Variability among subjects in displaying comprehension difficulties further com-
plicates generalization. However, employing careful cross-validation methods,
such as leave-one-subject-out cross-validation, recruiting more participants from
diverse backgrounds and experiences, and including more varied content, can
help validate model generalizability.
In summary, while these challenges highlighted the importance of careful pre-
processing, feature engineering, and validation techniques, the tools successfully
demonstrated their ability to utilize physiological measurements along with per-
sonal and behavioral features to predict both the quality of the code reviews in
iReview and language comprehension difficulties in iMind.
7.3 Future Directions
The work in this thesis has opened several possibilities for new research avenues
and future directions. To ensure that the work in this thesis is reproducible, the
following datasets are made publicly available to researchers.
• Chapter 2 and Chapter 6 datasets . The data set covers a wide
range of sensor readings, such as eye tracking with pupillography,
ECG, EDA and EEG with a 64-channel cap and
• Chapter 3 and Chapter 4 datasets . The data set covers a collec-
tion of sensors such as fMRI, ECG, EDA, and eye tracking. The
experiment aims to explore the cognitive state associated with
code reviews and bug identification.
For future directions of the TellBack approach, we categorize them into two
streams: application domain advancements and technique enhancements.
Starting with the application domain advancements, below are brief propos-
als:
Working with generative LLMs in Software Engineering As we envi-
sion future directions for this research, one promising avenue involves leveraging
biofeedback technology used in TellBack to assess comprehension difficulties in
human interactions with AI-generated software. Recent advances in generative
AI, particularly Large Language Models (LLMs), have opened up new possibilit-
ies but also introduced challenges related to understandability and trustworthi-
ness. Ensuring that users comprehend and trust the output produced by LLMs
is crucial for their review, testing, and maintainability.
By focusing on the cognitive load associated with understanding AI-generated
software using non-intrusive biofeedback techniques, which were developed in
this thesis, future research can address this challenge. Using biofeedback wear-
ables such as smartwatches and wristbands, it will be possible to measure cog-
nitive load and identify code regions that may be difficult to comprehend or
— 118 —

CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSIONS, CHALLENGES AND FUTURE WORK
confusing.
This method offers a promising way to gain deep understanding of the challenges
of comprehension in AI-generated software. This understanding can be valuable
for establishing a measure of trust for each section of the code produced by
LLMs. For example, it can help generate a comprehensive report highlighting
areas of code that are highly trustworthy because they are easily understood, as
well as areas that are less trustworthy due to their lack of clarity.
To advance this research, several key steps will be necessary. These include val-
idating the effectiveness of biofeedback techniques in real-world environments,
such as software companies, and developing methodologies for interpreting and
analyzing biofeedback data in the context of AI-generated code, and validat-
ing the understandability and trust metric with software developers, testers,
and quality engineers. I can summarize this future research in the following
points:
1. Assess the cognitive load associated with comprehending AI-generated
code using non-intrusive biofeedback techniques.
2. Identify and highlight regions of code that may be difficult to comprehend
or confusing.
3. Provide detailed reports on comprehension difficulties and coverage gaps
in the generated code.
4. Enhance the trust and maintainability of AI-generated code through met-
rics of understanding and trust.
I believe that this idea can be easily turned into a project that can be developed
to achieve the following milestones.
• Establishapilotlabandobtainethicalapprovalfornon-intrusivebiosensor
usage in estimating cognitive load and comprehension difficulties.
• Develop low-intrusive features for real-world implementation.
• Deploy low-intrusive hardware and setup in relevant environments.
• Validate framework in real-world scenarios to assess the meaningfulness
and soundness of understanding and trust metrics of the AI-generated
codes.
• Develop a roadmap for potential applications and possible exploitation
scenarios to guide software development teams in quality assurance, test-
ing, and maintainability efforts.
Working with other focused applications: The two primary applications
that the work described in this thesis focused on were natural language under-
standing and review, as well as code comprehension. Students, researchers, and
software engineers benefit from the tools created and are the subjects of invest-
igations. Nevertheless, there are several chances to apply the TellBack method
to different user groups, such as those who suffer from neurological or learning
disabilities like dyslexia or autism. Technology may have implications for how
— 119 —

CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSIONS, CHALLENGES AND FUTURE WORK
we evaluate the comprehension and engagement of those with these conditions
in order to try and offer the right kind of assistance. Additionally, training
and educational facilities can profit from this approach by implementing two
forms of interface: (a) tutor interface, which allows them to keep an eye on
students’ behavior and particularly difficult sections of the curriculum; and (b)
the trainee interface, which allows students to receive immediate and dynamic
support whenever they feel challenged or disengaged.
On the other hand, the advancements in the techniques can be as follows:
Exploring beyond cognitive load: Content comprehension is a complicated
process that not only includes mental processing or cognitive load, but also an
array of cognitive states. Cognitive states comprise several things, including fo-
cused attention, short-term memory, distraction, emotions, and motivation. To
go in this direction, it will be crucial to carry out well regulated studies, provide
carefully crafted stimuli to elicit a range of cognitive and affective states, and
then use machine learning or deep learning to identify the patterns associated
with each condition.
Contextual Support to Comprehension challenges: The work presented
in this thesis has built the models and instruments required to estimate and fore-
cast the comprehension challenges in digital content at the regional level. Yet,
it is strongly advised that further study be done to carry out the task of dy-
namically offering the required guidance to the users at the appropriate moment
and location on the screen when the user feels overwhelmed by the difficulty of
understanding a local section of the region. The support can be provided in a
number of ways, such as by employing generative AI to create new content or
summarize existing content, and to substitute difficult sections of content that
the tool was able to identify using TellBack technology in an adaptable manner.
Here, the new work must include user experience, user interface standards and
laws, and usability evaluation.
Lower intrusion and higher usabilityAlthoughthethesishasutilizedtheuse
of wearable wristbands, such as Empatica E4 and desktop eye-trackers, it is still
abigchallengetoselectthebesthardwaresetuptoensureitsapplicabilityamong
different work settings and across different industries. Therefore, future research
should focus on designing and optimizing software and hardware technologies to
ensure daily life technologies can be used to implement TellBack, such as using
commercial smartwatches and webcam eye-trackers instead of acquiring special
wearables or classical eye-trackers.
Additional advancements can be seen from the following angles:
Multimodal input data: through this approach, it is recommended to in-
clude multiple modalities, not only from the autonomic nervous system (ANS)
response, but also face gestures, audio, and other contextual and maybe en-
vironmental data, to capture a more comprehensive representation of content
comprehension. This will include analyzing how different modalities influence
cognitive and affective states and how they interact with each other.
Longitudinal studies: Conduct longitudinal studies to examine how cognit-
— 120 —

CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSIONS, CHALLENGES AND FUTURE WORK
ive and affective states evolve over time during content comprehension. This
can provide insight into learning processes, adaptation mechanisms, and the
effectiveness of interventions or learning strategies.
Individual Differences: Investigatehowindividualdifferences, suchasperson-
ality traits, prior knowledge, and learning styles, impact cognitive and affective
states during content comprehension. Understanding individual variability can
inform personalized learning approaches and interventions.
Ethical Considerations: Consider the ethical implications of using machine
learning or deep learning to analyze cognitive and affective states, particularly in
terms of privacy, data security, and potential biases. Develop ethical guidelines
and protocols for conducting research and implementing technology in this do-
main.
Since comprehension and understanding of content are fundamental to all indus-
tries and work environments, especially given the vast amount of information
we encounter on a daily basis, the implications and application areas are end-
less.

Appendix
A International Patent Application
Title: Method and system for scoring a software review process
Abstract: The following patent application outlines a novel approach for assess-
ing the quality of code reviews conducted by individual code reviewers. By lever-
aging biometrics and intelligent content comprehension techniques, this method
proposes a systematic way to score software review processes at the code line
level of granularity. The aim is to offer immediate feedback to code review-
ers, highlighting potential code lines that may have been inadequately covered
during the review process.
Application Details: PCT/IB2021/059461
Purpose: This appendix provides additional insights into the innovative meth-
odology developed as part of the research presented in the main thesis. By
including the patent application, readers gain a deeper understanding of the
practical applications and potential impact of the proposed approach in the
field of software development and review.
Note: The patent application document follows this introduction and offers
comprehensive details regarding the method and system described herein.
